<margin>
The harmony of
Astronomy &
Theology.
</margin>
Among all discerning, rational, and skillful philosophers <note>original: "Philosophis"</note>, both before and after the birth of Christ—indeed from the beginning of the world until this very hour—there has never been any doubt that the praiseworthy science of Astronomy is a right, true, and necessary art. Through it, in a wonderful manner, anyone can observe and explore all things past, present, and future without any falsehood or error. Since this is the case (as it is impossible to be otherwise), it follows naturally that it also corresponds with and perfectly aligns with true Theology. For, as the saying goes, "the straight and the true harmonize with all that is true" <note>original Latin: "Rectum etenim ac Verum, Vero cum consonat omni"</note>. This is clearly demonstrated in the explanation of heavenly and human creations; above all other sciences <note>original: "Wiessenschafften"</note>, Astronomy presents itself to Holy Theology—as if to its patron lady—as a willing and subservient handmaid. <margin>Romans 1</margin> For (as the Holy Apostle <note>Referring to St. Paul in his letter to the Romans</note> testifies), that which is known of God, God has revealed to them (the Gentiles), so that His invisible essence—that is, His eternal power and divinity—might be perceived by observing His works...

<summary>This page serves as a formal dedication to Augustus, Elector of Saxony, and introduces the argument that astronomy is a vital science that harmonizes perfectly with Christian theology.</summary>
<keywords>Augustus of Saxony, Astronomy, Theology, Natural Revelation, Philosophy, Elector, Holy Roman Empire</keywords>

from the creation of the world onward. Among all the innumerable visible creatures, the Divine, all-powerful, infinite, and incomprehensible Majesty is most especially shown and testified to us by the celestial bodies. 

<margin>Psalm 18 <note>In modern Bibles, this is Psalm 19.</note></margin>

<margin>

the firmament announces the work of His hands. Likewise: Praise the Lord, all stars and lights. And Holy Job praises the Lord along with the morning stars, etc.

Therefore, very elegantly and appropriately, **Astronomy** may be called a **Natural Theology**. For in the same way that the Higher Theology brings us to the knowledge of God through supernatural faith, so too this science—acting as the lower handmaid—is ready to serve her with a faithful hand. It provides us with guidance and leads us to a comprehension of the Divine Essence through natural reason. 

Hence, in his present work, this highly-praised **Theophrastus** <note>Referring to the author Paracelsus, whose full name was Theophrastus von Hohenheim.</note> remarkably titles it as a portion of Theology and the Word of God, along with Philosophy and Medicine. Indeed, he counts it among the theologians as a pillar of the Divine Word. From this, one may understand, see, and judge the time, hour, and person against whom we should guard ourselves. And likewise elsewhere, speaking of the arrival, origin, and properties of the Adamic Line <note>The lineage of humanity descending from Adam.</note>, he directs and draws all other faculties toward the

<margin>
In: *The Sagacious Philosophy of the Macrocosm and Microcosm* <note>original: "Sagaci Philosophia Macro & Microcosmi"</note>, that is, in *Astronomy*.
</margin>
foundation, fountainhead, and goal of Holy Theology with the following words:

> From what man was initially created, the Philosopher should know, and lay a cornerstone for the others; likewise the Theologian, so that he may distinguish between the Eternal and the Mortal, and not carelessly condemn him; likewise the Jurist, so that he does not judge him like a calf; likewise the Physician, so that he does not deliver him to the meat market like cattle. For when one well understands the "first matter" <term>materia prima: the fundamental, formless substance from which all things are created</term> of a thing, one also well understands its essence and properties. Holy Scripture states in that place:

It separates the masters from the apprentices and those who err in medicine, craftsmanship, and all kinds of arts.

It allows one to grasp and comprehend the proclamation of the stars within God's wonder-works, of the first and also of the second heaven. For it preserves many people from deception, so that within Astronomy, the ultimate goal of all arts may be grasped, measured, and understood—such as **medicine**, the study of **metals**, all **herbs**, and all **manual labor**.

# Preface.

and everything that is there: because through it is perceived and sought the innate disposition and mind of people. All children can and should be governed and live through and according to the right way—not according to fabricated delusion or the whims of the **Spirit of Giddiness** <note>original: "Spiritus Vertiginis"; a term for a spirit of confusion or deception</note>—but according to how **Astral Wisdom** is born through its **Ascendant** <term>Ascendant: The zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon at a specific moment, believed to influence a person's character and path.</term>. For the **Firmament** <note>The sphere of the stars and heavens.</note> is the **Natural Light**, and man has the **Firmament** from God. It is the natural cornerstone and perfection of all skill, the natural and essential Highest Good <note>original: "summum bonum"</note>; that is, our Father and Teacher instructs us in our current conduct—though only the mortal kind, without any mediation, from the nature of the stars—but divine conduct is taught to us by God alone from above. In these two lights (which are in no way contrary to each other, as has been claimed by sophists <note>Sophists: Here used to mean scholars who use clever but false or misleading arguments.</note> until now), we must lead our honorable lives and upright conduct in the natural light according to God’s will, and thus submit our natural body to the **Natural Light**.

Now, as necessary, pleasurable, and useful as the noble art of **Astronomy** proves itself to be—along with all its branches, such that it could almost be felt by one who is stone-blind—it has nevertheless been attacked with many insults, indignities, blasphemies, dishonors, harms, and slanders. Moreover, it has been stained, shadowed, and darkened by manifold errors and abuses. 

<margin>The neglect of Astronomy.</margin>

For first of all, this **Principal Science** has been robbed of its high, broad praise and dignity, and its nearly hundredfold usefulness, and has been driven and hidden away into a very narrow little nest or box. For a long time now, due to laziness, coarseness, and madness, only the single branch of **Astrology** has been held and taken for the whole. Thus the other parts are not only entirely omitted, but also mutilated, annihilated, laughed at, despised, rejected, and forcibly cast out from the **Synagogists' Chairs** <note>original: "Synagogisten Catheder"; likely a derogatory reference to the rigid, dogmatic academic pulpits of the time.</note> as if they were mere scraps, floor-cloths, or offcuts. From which it arises, that such **highly gifted**

<meta>This page continues the author's defense of astrology and natural philosophy. # Preface.

Many, and particularly notable arts, have fallen both into thoughtless
oblivion and away from their proper, orderly foundation and use.
These are the very arts in which all secrets and mysteries of the **stars** <note>original: "sidera"</note>
were undeniably hidden and contained, and through whose assistance,
management, and use, great and powerful **mysteries** <term>Arcana: Hidden or secret knowledge, often referring to the deeper mysteries of nature or alchemy.</term> were
exposed and discovered. While these were always esteemed by the **Ancient Sages**
as the very best and primary arts, they have—each and every one—
lately been regarded as nothing but abusive, superstitious,
sectarian, idolatrous, and hellish.

The artless, dull-witted, and delusional people have not
considered that every artistic skill—in the beginning, before it is seduced,
corrupted, and brought to ruin—was always laudable, righteous,
and faultless, pure and pleasing to God, and was discovered through God’s
providence and bestowal. It is only with time, through the misunderstanding,
presumption, and negligence of some, that they are drawn into harmful
and disadvantageous uses, as historians and naturalists
often confirm and demonstrate to us with many examples. These
same blunt, shallow, and coarse minds have also not
allowed that a foreknowledge of evil might be useful to us,
so that we may better avoid and flee from it. They fail to see that only
the evil, wicked, and scandalous *use* of a thing is damnable. As
is the case in these treatises, the author sufficiently refutes and
overturns such errors; and regarding this, **Trithemius** <note>Johannes Trithemius (1462–1516), a German abbot, polymath, and occultist.</note> says to the Count
of Westenburg, etc.: **The knowledge of evil is not evil,
but the use of it is.** <note>original: "Scientia mali non est malum, sed vfus."</note>

<margin>Contempt for and hindrances to Astrology.</margin>
But now, to speak of the remaining Astrology,
although no small honor is shown to it and no little utility
is attributed to it by its lovers and followers; nevertheless,
when one weighs and balances it against the blame it bears
because of the shamelessness, audacity, and ignorance of
**impostors**, as well as its own bareness and uncertainty—

<meta>catchword: because /</meta>

<summary>In this preface, the author defends astrology and other ancient "secret" arts against charges of superstition and heresy, arguing that these disciplines were originally divine gifts that have merely been corrupted by ignorant practitioners. He cites the scholar Johannes Trithemius to argue that knowing about "evil" or hidden things is not sinful, provided the knowledge is used correctly.</summary>

<keywords>astrology, ancient wisdom, Johannes Trithemius, arcana, corruption of knowledge, impostors, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Vorrede" (Preface) from the previous page, where the author was defending the ancient arts against those who call them superstitious. It transitions from a general defense to a specific list of the technical and philosophical difficulties that bring astrology into disrepute.</meta>

...is not entirely at fault, yet is constantly blamed, accused, and indicted; thus, through various dishonors, its honor and high praise are almost entirely obscured and extinguished. For (unfortunately all too true), many obstacles stand in its way; much criticism, suspicion, mockery, and unrest befalls it because of various doubts, wavering, and hesitations. These arise from the very swift and sudden **celestial conversion** <term>Celestial Conversion: The rapid rotation of the heavens (the *primum mobile*) that causes the positions of the stars to change constantly, making precise timing difficult.</term>, the doubtful point of our conception and birth, and the differences in the character and status of twins, or the inequality in mind, body, and wealth among men or women of the same age.

Furthermore, there are doubts regarding the unknown significance of certain obscure, dim stars; the uncertainty of the solar path and annual revolutions; the unsteady and changeable movement of **Mars** <note>original: "♂"</note> and **Mercury** <note>original: "☿"</note>; the flawed understanding of common **progression** <term>Progression: A predictive technique where the movement of the planets in the days following birth represents years in the person's life.</term>; and the inconsistent selection of **significators** <term>Significators: Specific planets or points in a birth chart that represent the person or a specific area of life, like health or career.</term> and **chronocrators** <term>Chronocrators: Literally "Time-Lords"; planets that rule over specific periods of a person's life.</term>, etc.

There are also the fleeting changes stemming from Divine providence through **Universal Influx** <term>Universal Influx: The macro-level astrological influence that affects the whole world at once, rather than an individual person.</term> in religion, along with war, hunger, plague, fire, shipwreck, earthquakes, the destruction of cities, the defeat of peoples, and the collapse of buildings. Likewise, there are the rare circumstances of regions, religious matters, statutes, customs, orders, parents, education, dealings, trades, place, and time—and lastly, the freedom and mobility of the human will, the instability of affairs and arts, and the like.

<margin>Page 52.</margin>
All this is easy to believe and not greatly to be wondered at, since Astrology is left almost naked and bare, and is nowhere properly taught or well-practiced with all its inherent thirteen members (presented here in only three), which are named as follows:

<margin>The thirteen members of Astrology.</margin>
->First, the Highest Mover.<- <note>original: "Primum, Summus Motor." This refers to the *Primum Mobile* or God as the primary cause of all motion in the universe.</note>
->Second, the Course of the Stars.<- <note>original: "Secundum, Stellarum cursus."</note>
->Third, the Nature of the Firmament.<- <note>original: "Tertium, Firmamenti natura."</note>
->Fourth, the Operation of the Stars.<- <note>original: "Quartum, Astrorum operatio."</note>

# Preface.

These especially famous arts have both fallen into thoughtless oblivion and have strayed from their proper, orderly foundation and practice. These are the very arts in which all secrets and mysteries of the **stars** <note>original: "sidera"</note> were undeniably hidden and contained. Through their assistance, administration, and use, powerful **secrets** <note>original: "Arcana"</note> were revealed and discovered. While they were always esteemed by the ancient sages as the very best and primary of all, they have lately—collectively and individually—been regarded as nothing but abusive, superstitious, sectarian, idolatrous, and hellish.

The artless, dull-witted, and insane people have not considered that every skill, at its beginning—before it is seduced, corrupted, and brought to ruin—was always invented as something laudable, just, blameless, pure, and pleasing to God, through God's providence and granting. It is only over time, through the misunderstanding, presumption, and negligence of some, that these arts have been drawn into harmful and disadvantageous practices, as historians and naturalists often assure and demonstrate to us with many examples. 

These same dull, slow-witted, and coarse-headed people have also not allowed that the foreknowledge of evil is useful to us, so that we may better avoid and flee from it. They fail to see that only the wicked and damnably offensive use of such knowledge is truly condemnable. As the author sufficiently refutes and nullifies such errors in these treatises, **Trithemius** <note>Johannes Trithemius (1462–1516) was a German Benedictine abbot and a polymath often cited in occult and astrological texts for his work on cryptography and "angel magic."</note> says to the **Count of Westenburg** <note>original: "Grauen von Vvestenburg"</note>, etc.: **"Knowledge of evil is not evil; it is the use of it that is evil."** <note>original Latin: "Scientia mali non est malum, sed usus."</note>

<margin>
The contempt for and hindrances of Astrology.
</margin>
Now, however, to speak of what remains of **Astrology**: although it still shows no small honor, and no little usefulness is attributed to it by its devotees and followers, yet if one weighs and balances it against the blame it carries—due to the impudence, foolhardiness, and ignorance of impostors, as well as its own nakedness and uncertainty—

<summary>The author defends ancient occult arts and astrology, arguing that they were originally God-given and pure, though later corrupted by human ignorance. He cites the scholar Trithemius to argue that knowing about evil is not a sin, but using that knowledge for evil is.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, Trithemius, occult secrets, defense of magic, corruption of arts, foreknowledge, Westenburg</keywords>

# Preface.

...[rejected], yet not entirely, nor always rightfully, accused and indicted, so that through many kinds of dishonor, their honor and high praise are almost completely darkened and extinguished. For (alas, it is all too true) many obstacles stand in the way; much criticism, suspicion, mockery, and reproach befalls them because of manifold doubts, instabilities, wavering, and vacillating. These arise from the very swift and sudden <term>conversion</term> <note>The rapid rotation or shifting of the celestial spheres.</note> of the heavens; from the doubtful point of our conception and birth; from the unequal customs and states of twins; and from the inequality of spirit, body, and possessions among men or women of the same age. 

Likewise, there are doubts from the unknown significance of certain obscure, dark stars; from the uncertainty of the Sun's path and annual <term>revolutions</term> <note>The Solar Return; a chart cast for the moment the Sun returns to its natal position to predict the year ahead.</note>; from the unsteady and changeable movement of the Sun and Mercury; from the precarious understanding of common <term>progression</term> <note>A predictive technique where planets are moved forward in the chart according to a specific time-key.</note>; from the inconsistent selection of <term>significators</term> <note>The specific planets or points designated to represent the person or a specific area of life, like wealth or health.</note> and <term>chronocrators</term> <note>Literally "time-lords"; planets that rule over specific periods of a person's life.</note>, and so on; and from the discordant final judgments of the professors. 

Furthermore, there are the transient changes stemming from Divine providence through universal influence on religion—bringing war, hunger, pestilence, fire, shipwreck, earthquakes, the destruction of cities, the defeat of peoples, and the collapse of buildings. Likewise, there are the strange circumstances of various regions, religious matters, statutes, customs, orders, parents, education, deeds, trades, place, and time. And lastly, there is the freedom and mobility of the human will, the instability of affairs and arts, and the like. 

<margin>Page 52.</margin>
All this is easy to believe, and not greatly to be wondered at, because astrologers are left almost entirely naked and bare, and are nowhere properly taught and well-practiced in all their thirteen peculiar components (compiled here in only three), which are as follows:

<margin>Thirteen members of Astrology.</margin>

Furthermore, one would see how they fulfill their effects at <term>Conception</term> and constitute the <term>animalistic man</term> <note>Referring to the physical, "souled" body (from the Latin 'anima') that responds to biological and celestial influences, as opposed to the higher spiritual intellect.</note> through the <term>Astral Conjunction</term> and concordance; by this means, much misfortune and ruin could be avoided, etc.

But (as happens in all things), through the base, abominable contempt and neglect of the rulers, they have sunk into a deep, deficient, and ugly oblivion. Such significant...

# Preface.

<margin>The essence of man.</margin> <note>original Latin: "Hominis essentia."</note>
For these reasons, truly, truly, not in a piecemeal, split, divided, or shattered way, but the whole, entire Astronomy <note>In this period, "Astronomy" and "Astrology" were often used interchangeably to describe the study of celestial influences on Earth.</note> shall be thoroughly investigated: for it was made, given, and discovered for the benefit, fruitfulness, and prosperity of the human race. We ought to explore and experience it, since it primarily concerns us alone to know ourselves well, both from the inside and the outside—who and what we are, what is to be maintained within us, and what is to be cast away from each person—so that Eternal Wisdom and Truth may cultivate and administer it all. This is to say: that man is formed by the hand of God in His image, a <term>Microcosm</term> <note>From the Greek for "little world"; the idea that man contains within himself a miniature version of the entire universe.</note> or the "Fifth Essence of the World," <note>original Latin: "Quinta Mundi essentia."</note> drawn from the mass or "Great Composite" of all things and simple elements of the universal machinery of the world—that is, from the <term>Macrocosm</term> <note>original Latin: "Macro vel Megalocosmo"; the "great world" or the universe at large.</note>. Man is indeed the Medium and Center of all creatures, both celestial and terrestrial; a "two-bodied animal," <note>original Latin: "Animal bicorpor."</note> fused together from a sensible or elementary body and an insensible or starry body <note>original Latin: "Sidereo corpore." This refers to the "astral body" that connects the physical flesh to the heavens.</note>.

Furthermore, we should consider alertly, diligently, and with earnestness that the principal reason for our formation is to be the workers and followers of Nature. To ensure we can apply her and put her to work, we are shaped after and formed as an image of the Holy, unfathomable Trinity. Our true understanding lies in our supernatural, eternal Divine image, not in the other two transitory images—those governed by the elements and the constellations. Yet, despite such great benefit and necessity, this glorious, precious, highly estimable, and praiseworthy *Science* <note>original: "Scienz."</note> has been held as suspicious, hateful, shameful, and punishable—primarily by theologians during the time of the growing Christian Church, and also by highly-regarded astronomers—and...

<summary>This page argues that man is a "microcosm" containing both physical and celestial elements, and that the study of astronomy is essential for self-knowledge and fulfilling man's role as a divine "worker of nature." It defends the science against historical theological and academic criticism.</summary>

# Preface.

<margin>The fatal causes of events.</margin> <note>original Latin: "Fatales euētumm causæ."</note>
...was esteemed dishonorable and cast to the wind (as is proven by that awkwardly composed title, *On Mathematicians and Sorcerers, etc.*) <note>original Latin: "De Mathematicis & Maleficis." In Roman law, the term "mathematicus" was often used synonymously with "astrologer" or "sorcerer," reflecting a period where the practice was legally suspect.</note> until such time as the Christian faith increased, and its prophecies drawn from Nature were found to agree and be true when compared with theological prophecies. This [previous dishonor], however, was caused solely by the misunderstanding, audacity, and excess of the professors [of this art], who declared the stars to be ordained, constrained, and unavoidable causes of all future accidents and histories through God’s eternal and incomprehensible providence—or who otherwise mixed in much superstition. They extended and elevated the limits of sidereal power <note>The power or influence of the stars.</note> over our free will and over supernatural and divine predestination. Yet, even without this, if natural forces were not overvalued, the Holy Scripture would rightly be compared with the art of the stars, following the example of the God-loving patriarchs and prophets, such as Noah, Abraham, Joseph, Moses, Daniel, David, Solomon, etc., **confirming supernatural inspiration through natural investigation.** <note>original Latin: "supernaturalem Inspirationem per naturalem inuestigationem confirmando."</note>

<margin>The wise man rules the stars: with piety as guide, and virtue as companion.</margin> <note>original Latin: "Sapiens dominatur Astris: Pietate duce, Virtute comite." This was a popular Renaissance maxim suggesting that while stars influence the body, the soul remains free.</note>
Those clumsy, irrational minds have never considered that the wise man rules over the stars; that is, he survives the animal wit and the powers of the senses—which is the image of the Earth that passes away. That is to say: the man who lives in the image [of God] out of God’s wisdom, he dominates over the elemental and starry body. Nevertheless, he should uphold and satisfy both images; every way of life should be found in the law of the Lord, in Nature, in God’s will, and in the Divine Spirit. He should not, through his own cleverness, place the mortal, sinful body before the eternal image and sanctuary. Accordingly, we will then, according to the immortal and God-pleasing image, be...

# Preface.

<margin>Gods and Sons of God.</margin> <note>original Latin: "Dij & Filij Dei."</note>
<margin>Sacred Theology</margin> <note>original Latin: "s. Theologia."</note>
We are called Gods and Sons of the Most High <note>original Latin: "Dij & Filij Altissimi." This refers to Psalm 82:6, a common citation in Renaissance philosophy to suggest that humans carry a spark of divine intellect.</note> because the <term>Light of Nature: "Naturæ Lumen," a concept suggesting that God granted humans an innate, natural intelligence to understand the physical world</term> is hidden within us, and that Light is God. Therefore, our Knowledge and Arts are divine arts and wisdom; and these are within our power and might, for they enter into us at the moment of our conception, etc. Therefore, in conclusion: those things which are criticized, disparaged, or called into question by some—including currently prominent and respected men—out of madness and rashness, should in no way be diminished, despised, or discarded. Otherwise, Philosophy, Law, Medicine, the art of Agriculture, trade, military skill, etc.—and even the highest treasure of human nature, the princess and queen, the mother of all good arts and understandings, the sole duchess of our life and the peace and best delight of our mind, **Sacred Theology**—as well as all human sustenance, business, and practice, would have to suffer for this accusation, however innocent they may be.

To further release, uphold, pacify, and protect this innocence and truth, Most Illustrious, High-born Prince-Elector, etc., Most Gracious Lord, I have been moved to especially dedicate this *Astronomical Book of T.H.* <note>The initials likely refer to Theophrastus von Hohenheim, better known as Paracelsus, the famous physician and occultist.</note> to your Electoral Grace. > For astronomers, it serves to reveal and use the entire power of the firmament; and this is especially fitting for the heads of this world more than for the common man, so that much may be prevented that otherwise falls to ruin through such ignorance.

# Preface.

<margin>The four kinds of Astronomy or external Tinctures</margin> <note>original Latin: "4. genera Astron. uel extraneae Tincturae"</note>
The four general kinds of astronomy, sciences, or external <term>Tinctures</term> <note>In alchemy and hermetic philosophy, a "tincture" is a spiritual or physical essence that can transform or "color" a substance or person.</note> and influences harmonize and operate within man. It is appropriate that they are called "Influences," for their operations correspond with one another, even though no one kind is identical to the other—though they are similar. Instead, each kind unites, harmonizes, and imprints itself upon man according to its own nature, essence, and specific property.

There are, however, throughout only nine parts <note>namely: Divination in place of Necromancy, and the Manual as the final species, substituted for Adept Mathematics; likewise, to those seven members are added the two remaining: one of which is Adept Philosophy, which contains the knowledge of celestial powers in the elements and elemental things, and also the science of Composition; the other is Adept Medicine, which encompasses the knowledge of celestial diseases and celestial cures, about which the Author treats elsewhere at length</note> <note>original Latin: "scil. Diuinatione loco Necromantiæ, ac Manuali vltima specie, pro Mathematica Adepta repositis: Item ad illa 7. Autor alibi ex professo tractat"</note> through which these nine—the four astronomical <term>Genera</term> <note>Categories or kinds. The author notes that the last of these is the "Satanic" kind, which must be avoided by the faithful</note>—must be accomplished and used by man as a means. They differ only in that each has a particular use, yet they arrive at one end and one path, etc.; more on this at another place and time.

G." / "Chur-Fürstliche Gnaden." This refers to a high-ranking prince of the Holy Roman Empire who held the right to vote for the Emperor.</note> most merciful <term>Munificence</term> <note>Generosity and noble liberality</note>; and because since that time I have had no opportunity to prove my lack of forgetfulness and my gratitude toward Your Prince-Electoral Grace in any way as is fitting, other than now with this <term>Work</term> <note>original Latin: "Opere"</note>, I have therefore not wished, dared, nor been obliged to carelessly neglect this opportunity. ## Preface.

<margin>Isaiah 49</margin> <note>Isaiah 49:23: "Kings shall be your foster fathers, and their queens your nursing mothers." This was a common verse used to justify the role of political leaders in supporting the church and scholarship.</note>
It is my most humble, modest, and diligent request and petition that Your Electoral Grace might graciously accept this work of T.H. <note>referring to Theophrastus Paracelsus</note> with pleasant and gracious favor and satisfaction. As a lover and promoter of justice, truth, and wisdom (according to the lovely and blessed exhortation of the Prophet: *Kings shall be your foster fathers, and their princes your nursing mothers*), and for the praise of God's name, the fruitfulness of all Germany and Christendom, and the service of all your lands, I ask that you graciously favor, promote, and expand such well-grounded and reliable studies. Toward Your Electoral Grace, I remain most humbly and obediently of good will and mind, tireless and eager at all times to prove my service. May God the Lord of all hosts—the beginning and the end, the all-in-all, the unique, infinite, and almighty essence of all things—grant and preserve for Your Electoral Grace a peaceful reign, long-lasting health of body and mind, and the eternal salvation of the soul. Given in the Imperial Free City of Cologne on the Rhine, on the 17th of January, in the year 1567.

Castriotus"</note>, called Skanderbeg on account of his virtues, Duke of the Epirotes, died in God in the year of Christ 1466, aged 63, after defending Epirus and Hungary from the Turks for 24 years; likewise the Emperor Theodosius, etc., died in God in Milan <note>original: "Mediolani"</note> in the year of Christ 398, in the 16th year of his reign, aged 60).

->Most humble and obedient,<-

->Balthassar Flœter<-
->of Sagan.<- <note>Saganus indicates Flœter was from Sagan, a town in Silesia, modern-day Żagań, Poland.</note>

<summary>In this concluding section of the preface, Balthassar Flœter formally dedicates his work to an unnamed Elector, positioning the study of Paracelsian science as a service to both God and the German nation. He dates the letter January 17, 1567, linking the date to the historical deaths of the anti-Ottoman hero Skanderbeg and the Roman Emperor Theodosius.</summary>

### ON THEOPHRASTUS PARACELSUS

->Theophrastus was such in body, a second Apollo;<-
->But Apelles <note>original: "Apelles"; the most celebrated painter of ancient Greece, used here to suggest that even the greatest artist could not capture Paracelsus’s inner spirit</note> could hardly depict the greatness of his heart.<-

->On every side you see pious words and golden sayings;<-
->While he lived, he always carried these in his heart and upon his lips.<-

# THE IMAGE: A POEM.

With this man as our leader, we shall follow Nature and God as our only
Teachers, and not your pleasing whims, O <term>Cenotechne</term> <note>original: "Cenotechne"; likely a derogatory term derived from the Greek *kenos* (empty) and *techne* (art/skill), referring to the "empty arts" or "vain theories" of Paracelsus's academic rivals</note>.
From God he drew his beginning, his middle, and his end,
Committing himself and all his own to God alone.
He established the Holy Bible as the straight rule for his
Writings, which serve as monuments of a pious mind:
Under the "husk" of these writings, he perceived heavenly fruits,
Fruits which did not remain hidden from his eyes.
Finally, he knew the inner secrets of Mother Nature:
That she creates all things from <term>Azoth</term>, Salt, and Sulphur <note>The *Tria Prima* or Three Primes. Paracelsus taught that all matter was composed of these three principles: the soul (Sulphur), the body (Salt), and the spirit (Mercury/Azoth)</note>.
Here he well-established four Columns with learned art,
And placed them as stone supports for all the Wise:
So that every matter, pleasant and useful for us to know,
Might be encountered as plain and certain, through faith.
He proved no medicines by mere belief, but as true by the senses,
Even if the salvation of the Soul rests in Hope and Faith.
For as a faithful <term>Machaon</term> <note>original: "Machaon"; in Greek mythology, the son of Asclepius and the physician to the Greeks during the Trojan War</note> to the people and to leaders,
He himself often brought relief with Machaonian aid.
Why do I linger? He was learned, truthful, and not at all proud,
Ever ready to bring welcome aid to the poor:
Sincere and just, and with pure morals, he duly
Completed the entire journey of a nourishing life.
Therefore, you <term>Theophrastomaſtiges</term> <note>original: "THeophraſtomaſtiges"; meaning "the scourgers of Theophrastus," a term used for the fierce critics who attacked Paracelsus's reputation</note> with your viperous mouths,
Do not tear at this innocent man, you harmful ones.
For such a great light, let thanks be given to the Highest Physician,
And may He grant others with a genius similar to yours, O Theophrastus.

->PHILALETHES f.<- <note>A pseudonym meaning "Lover of Truth." The "f." stands for *fecit*, meaning "Philalethes made [this poem]"</note>

<summary>A laudatory poem defending Paracelsus's character and methods, framing him as a pious physician who followed God and Nature rather than the "empty arts" of his contemporaries. It highlights his core theories, including the three chemical principles and the four pillars of medicine.</summary>
<keywords>Theophrastus Paracelsus, Tria Prima (Azoth, Salt, Sulphur), Four Pillars of Medicine, Machaon, Cenotechne, Theophrastomaſtiges, Philalethes, medical philosophy</keywords>

# PROPHETESSES

And <term>Sibyls</term> <note>original: Sibyllen; ancient female prophetesses believed to be possessed by a deity</note> also have their basis, for it is a creation of God; yet it does not concern us, but rather 
<margin>Five species of prophecies <note>original Latin: Vaticiniorū species quinque</note>.</margin>
the righteousness of God alone. There are five kinds of prophecy, each with its own specific forms:

1. Is that of the Prophets and Sibyls; it is the most foundational and proceeds from God, and not from any art or by good fortune or chance; therefore they do nothing of themselves. 

2. Is the prophecy of Nature; that is, that Nature herself, through <term>signs</term> <note>original Latin: signa</note>, indicates her own misfortune to us at her end—that is, when she is about to meet an evil end. 

3. <term>Divination</term> <note>original Latin: Diuinatio</note> comes from man out of the invention of his own <term>light</term> <note>Paracelsus often refers to the "Light of Nature," the innate human capacity for reason and discovery</note> and the discovery of where a matter is headed. 

4. <term>Sortilege</term> <note>original Latin: Sortilegium; the practice of foretelling the future by drawing lots or through the influence of spirits</note> is the foretelling of spirits, according to how man makes an image with them, or according to how they truly grant him knowledge of a thing. 

5. <term>Augury</term> <note>original Latin: Auguriū</note> is derived from animals and creatures, whereby one recognizes through them what is about to happen to man—floods of water, winds, etc.

Prophet, Sibyl, and Apostle are on the same level <note>original Latin: sunt in vno gradu</note>, for they 
<margin>True and divine seers <note>original Latin: veri & diui vates</note>.</margin>
proceed from the one mouth of God <note>original Latin: ex vno ore Dei</note>; they have two parts: for comfort and for punishment. Comfort is the task of the Prophets and Sibyls: the announcement of the Messiah. And these types must proceed from God the Father; everything like should proceed from its like. Likewise, the prophecies of the <term>Last Judgment</term> <note>original Latin: vltimi iudicij</note> are also for their comfort and must also be taken from the Prophets, Christ, and the Apostles. For punishment, it is twofold: 1. in this life <note>original Latin: in hac vita</note>, such as the destruction of kingdoms, <term>upheavals</term> <note>original Latin: mutationes</note>, etc.; and 2. regarding the Last Judgment.

<sig>B ij</sig>
<meta>catchword: Such</meta>

<summary>Paracelsus defines and ranks five distinct types of prophecy, distinguishing divine revelation from natural signs and human divination. He explains that the highest forms of prophecy—those of the Prophets, Sibyls, and Apostles—serve the dual purpose of offering comfort through the Messiah and warning of divine judgment.</summary>

<keywords>prophecy, Sibyls, divination, sortilege, augury, divine revelation, Last Judgment, types of foresight, Paracelsian philosophy</keywords>

# FROM THE BOOK OF ART

All such things proceed only from God and Scripture <note>original Latin: *ex Deo & Scriptura*</note>: From God, for those among His own to whom He wishes to grant it. False interpretations concerning the Last Judgment <note>original Latin: *de ultimo iudicio*</note> are all founded upon signs—that is, when one least thinks of it and it is most sudden—but still in this life <note>original Latin: *in hac vita adhuc*</note> certain destructions are seen, as in the Old Testament; however, the time is known to God alone. Therefore, those who speak of such things in simplicity, and do not understand themselves, are to be highly esteemed as speaking from the mouth of God; no other art is capable of this. These people are the three: <term>Prophets</term>, <term>Sibyls</term> <note>ancient female prophetesses believed to be divinely inspired</note>, and Disciples of Christ. These are by their nature of a simple character, not greedy for honor or wealth, etc., and for this reason they are despised by the worldly-wise—that is, hearing and seeing they do not see and do not hear <note>original Latin: *audientes & videntes non vident, non audiunt*</note>; thus God will have it.

# PRESAGES

<sig>B iij</sig>
<meta>catchword: -tia (continuing from "acciden-")</meta>

[They] know [all things] before they happen, even until the end <note>original Latin: *sciunt, antequam fiat, usque ad finem*</note>. But because
<margin>Deceptive demons <note>original Latin: *Demones fallaces*</note>.</margin>
God has made them mutes and liars, they may not show such things to mankind as clearly as they know them, for God does not wish it; yet, whatever they are able and willing to grant to humans, they do reveal. Therefore, such arts are violently false; there is something to them, but no firm foundation. One should believe the spirits only so far and no further; what they happen to get right is rare and doubtful.

<margin>Natural [signs] <note>original Latin: *Naturales*</note>.</margin>
From Nature, it is thus: the Heavens have their course and journeying, like a pilgrim traveling across the land, and through long experience, one has become aware of these courses and what they effect at this or that place, or in this or that time. Just as the Moon’s path goes from one place to another—at one place it works upon this, turning it into that; at another place, it works upon something else. Like a steady mule that does not go before a blacksmith unless one hammers on its irons <note>referring to horseshoeing</note>: so may one understand such things. It is the same with other stars, and with the seasons of the year <term>anni</term> <note>Latin: "of the year"</note>, Summer and Winter, and what each one produces. If one knows these things, then the Concordance <note>the harmonious agreement between celestial cycles and earthly events</note> allows one to understand what kind of accidents <term>accidentia</term> <note>in this context, "accidents" refers to outward events or non-essential changes that happen to things</note> will occur at this or that time. It is just like
<margin>The Traveler <note>original Latin: *Viator*</note>.</margin>
a journey on Earth: at a certain place, one knows all the various accidents <term>accidentibus</term> and other things that occur until one arrives there, as acciden-

# 6 FROM THE BOOK OF ART

...accidents <note>referring to the conclusion of "accidentia" from the previous page</note> of walking or riding, whether arising from oneself or the object. From oneself: becoming lame or not. From the object: whether travel is by land or water. Likewise, stopping at an inn, hearing or seeing new tidings, and so on. Also, the presages or proclamations derived therefrom: that this and that will happen, for a beginning has already been made toward this or that end. In the same way, the Heavens are to be recognized, for they also proceed according to their Nature. He who knows this may present the **Pilgrims of the Heavens** <note>a term for the planets, which "wander" or "pilgrimage" across the fixed stars</note> to the people. Likewise, in Heaven there are many dwellings, none like another, and many strange day-works and journeys within it. He who experiences much therein knows how to herald many natural things for the coming year. Some have a

Human nature, Limbal and Spiritual.

basis—as they claim—from the **Ascendants** <term>Ascendenten</term> <note>the zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of birth</note>. # PRAESAGAE. 7

...meet by chance / or not / that is / whether they went out from home together or not / and found him on the way / who knows not where to go / and though he had nothing planned / they persuade him to travel there with them / and thus he enters their company / and manner and character / that is now one way.

Education and Instruction alter nature. <note>original Latin: "Educatio & Institutio alterant naturam."</note>
</margin>

The other is / that the child *by accident* <note>original: "ex accidente," meaning by chance circumstance or external event rather than inherent nature</note> is raised in various ways by the parents or **teachers** <term>præceptoribus</term> <note>from the Latin for instructors or tutors</note>: thus his natural **disposition** <term>wäderschafft</term> <note>an archaic term referring to one's innate temperament or constitution</note> is drawn onto another path / and the first is broken / as commonly happens with children / for one seldom raises them according to their Nature. Now their Nature is difficult to recognize or predict / upon this many **Nativities** <term>Nativitet</term> <note>astrological birth charts</note> are made / which encompass not only the natural **pilgrimage** <note>the life path dictated by the stars</note> alone / but also the human guidance / such as parents, **teachers** <term>Præceptores</term>, and so on / whereby luck and misfortune are also indicated. But such is not natural art / but comes from the **Spirits** <term>Geistern</term> / who reveal such things / namely thus: First, this **Astrologer** <term>Astrologus</term> uses the judgment of the pilgrimage from the stars, showing with what luck and misfortune / poverty or wealth / they lead the child / if the child follows them. Beyond this, there are Spirits in Heaven / who mingle with the stars / and run alongside them / just as a human has his own **attending spirit** <term>Nebengeist</term> <note>a companion spirit or "genius" believed to follow a person through life</note> with him / that is / they also strive to be pilgrims / but in such a way that they lead the child onto other paths than the stars are accustomed to / that is according to their own will / for they

->rest<- <note>original: "feiren" - likely part of a sentence continuing to the next page, such as "do not rest."</note>

# FROM THE BOOK OF ART

...[the Spirits] do not rest for a single moment with us, even from the time of birth. Now, one must pay close attention to such a Spirit, which is mixed in with the **Ascendant** <term>Ascendente</term> <note>the sign of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of birth, believed to influence personality</note>; for this Spirit will exert more influence than the stars themselves. For in such an Ascendant, arising from these spirits, are found all kinds of evil, as well as the invention and design of vices: fraud, skill in wicked deeds, idolatry, false witness, lying, cheating, murder, stealing, cursing, blasphemy, warring, whoring, drinking, and so forth. For the nature inherent in the stars does not instill such things into human beings.

Now, whoever possesses the knowledge, condition, and qualities of this Spirit within himself, he may create a good **Nativity** <term>Natiuitet</term> <note>a birth chart or horoscope</note>. For there is an art, **Astronomy** <term>Astronomia</term> and **Astrology** <term>Astrologia</term>, which speak of these things and attribute them to the stars, such as the Moon <note>original Latin: "Lunae"</note>, Mars <note>original: "Marti"</note>, and so on; but that is not the case.

<margin>
**False Astrologers** <note>original: "Pseudastrologi"</note>
</margin>

They say, "this person is of the nature of Mars," even though it is actually the nature of the Spirit within him. One may well call them by such names, provided one does not mean the actual stars; for such is the way with the course of the heavens. Has the **Devil** <term>Diabolus</term> not cleverly patched himself into Astronomy and Astrology? He allows himself to be seen as the stars, so that **judgments** <term>iudicia</term> <note>astrological predictions or assessments of a person's character</note> are based upon him. He can hide himself so masterfully that because people now place so much importance upon it, he has secretly breathed his own influence into them. They did not intend this, but instead attributed everything to the heavens—to its **houses** <term>domibus</term> <note>the twelve divisions of the celestial sphere in a horoscope</note>, its **aspects** <term>aspectibus</term> <note>the specific angles between planets</note>, its **conjunctions** <term>Coniunction</term> <note>when two celestial bodies appear in the same position from Earth</note>, and so on. But through these things, the spirits have suggested to them that without this...

# PREDICTORS

...[the Spirits] have it in their minds to lead children astray in such a way. Therefore, the art was once righteous, but has been corrupted by the **Devil** <term>Diabolus</term>, who, like an ape <note>The "Ape of God" (*Simia Dei*) is a traditional concept describing the Devil as a creature who clumsily mimics God's works to deceive humanity.</note>, always has his own followers: just as there are false prophets, there are also false **Astronomers** <term>Astronomos</term>, and likewise in the elements there are false **Physicists** <term>Physicos</term>, and so forth. For this reason, if we did not involve ourselves in such matters, the Devil would have no place among us. If we ever wish to know what Nature is unable to provide, the Devil allows himself to be employed, even without our knowing; for just as he can possess a person openly, he can also do so secretly. Therefore, you wicked **Physician** <note>original Latin: "Physice nequam"</note> and Pharisee, desist from such arts and hold fast to the **Word of God** <note>original Latin: "Verbi Dei"</note>.

### Second Treatise

<margin>
Where the four types of fortune-tellers originate:
</margin>

There are four arts of the **Fortune-tellers** <term>Sortilegi</term> <note>diviners or those who cast lots to predict the future</note>. These arose from the fact that the Spirits are invisible and cannot speak; because of this, they placed such arts into the **Imagination** of men. Thus, men invented these things—not from the **human or natural light** <note>original Latin: "Lumine humano aut naturali," referring to innate reason or the light of nature</note>, but from the promptings of the Spirits. These spirits acted as the first **inventors of these arts** <note>original Latin: "Inuentores harum artium"</note>. They were possessed by the spirits, and out of that possession they devised these practices, and then the students were taught by those inventors. Some have attributed this to God, but that is not so; God does not want such arts from...

# FROM THE BOOK OF ART

<meta>catchword: falls</meta>

...[the spirits] should not have us, but rather we should live in His commandments. Although it is true that these **Spirits** <term>Geyste</term> come from God and not from the Devil, they are forbidden from teaching us such things. Yet they do not desist, and for this reason God punishes them so that they reveal no complete truth, but only that which is doubtful and mendacious. This is "stument" <note>likely meaning "stunted" or "indistinct," from the German *stumpf* or *stumm*</note>—that is, half true and half false. The fact that they are now disobedient to God is done solely to lead humanity into **Idolatry** <term>Abgötterey</term>, so that man also acts against God's commandments, where He says that one should not worry about tomorrow, and so forth <note>A reference to Matthew 6:34: "Therefore do not worry about tomorrow, for tomorrow will worry about itself."</note>.

<margin>
Geomancy.
</margin>
Now, **Geomancy** <term>Geomantia</term> <note>A method of divination where patterns are made by casting earth, pebbles, or marking points at random in the sand or on paper.</note> is the art of points with sixteen signs and figures. These are arranged according to their kind and nature as if they truly existed within Nature itself. Along with this, it teaches how to govern the **transfers** <note>original Latin: *Translationes*, a technical term for how influence moves from one figure to another</note>, the "joys" <note>original: *freidẽ* (Freuden), referring to the favorable positions of signs</note>, the form, the points, and the entire figure, for which rules are also provided. Now, the spirits guide the practitioner's hand so that he makes as many points as are needed until a **judgment** <note>original Latin: *iudicium*</note> is reached concerning the matter at hand. The spirits know well how many points must stand so that a figure is formed which indicates the answer; thus, they guide the hand correctly.

<margin>
A Geomantic Example.
</margin>
For example: suppose I wanted to know what color coat the man outside the door is wearing. The spirit assigns the seven colors to different signs, and to each one a geomantic sign. Then he teaches me how to make the figure. The spirit knows the color, but I do not understand it; he guides my hand so that I record many points in a line and strike them off <note>The process of "striking off" pairs of points to see if the remainder is odd (one point) or even (two points) is the core mechanic of geomancy.</note> until the color...

Afterwards, **Superstition** <term>Superstitio</term> was added to it, so that the spirit might sufficiently lead man astray: whether you do it at night or under a beautiful sky; pray first, and afterwards say nothing, and so forth. Their attempts at "improving" the practice are just like the art itself <note>meaning these rituals are as hollow as the divination they support</note>. The **Ascendant** <note>original Latin: *Ascendens*; the sign rising on the eastern horizon</note> is twofold: the star and the spirit. The star possesses the art of **Astronomy** <term>Astronomia</term>, while the spirit possesses **Pyromancy** <term>Pyromantia</term> <note>The art of divination by fire or through fiery spirits</note>. He who now has a favorable spirit, and who does not want to trust God, makes for a good **sortilege-caster** <term>Sortilegist</term> <note>One who practices sortilege, or divination by drawing lots, often involving the casting of small objects or pebbles</note>. He who has a lying spirit is the opposite; for the spirit is more bound to one person than to another, and therefore more of a liar than the other. Therefore, where one **sortilege-caster** is better than the other, it is not a matter of skill, but of the spirit involved. It is also a **Satanic trick** <term>Satanischer griff</term> that he [the Devil] strives to create enmity and envy among such practitioners, so that the poor human being is sufficiently plagued and does not recognize that the art, over which there is such envy, is nothing but patchwork and nothing certain. For example, if I have a **sortilege-caster** perform a reading for me regarding a woman, to see how she will turn out, and he speaks ill of her to me, and I do not take her, but instead take another—then I must still wait to see how it turns out on both sides. # FROM BOOK 2 OF THE ART
<note>original Latin: *EX LIB. 2 ARTIS*</note>

One observes how a thing appears to the eyes—as some see figures in the air at night, or when figures appear in the heavens that signify something. Similarly, one sometimes sees people floating in the air, the clanging of armor, or rumbling. Sometimes one sees such shadows in the water, in **crystals** <term>Crystallen</term>, **beryls** <term>Beryllen</term> <note>Both crystals and beryls were used as scrying stones to glimpse the future or hidden secrets</note>, and likewise in mirrors, or upon the thumbs and fingernails <note>A practice known as onychomancy, where oil or wax was applied to a person's nails to create a reflective surface for divination</note>, and so forth.

The cause lies in the **spirits** <term>Geistern</term>—in what they wish to present or not, whether within you or in another. Whatever a person desires from them, they teach a **superstition** <term>Superstition</term> for it, such that the person must pray or conjure them with force, and so on, as if they would not act otherwise. They do this so that **man** <term>homo</term> <note>Latin: human being</note> might believe he is compelling them through God—yet God does not desire this. The spirit is well pleased when things go badly. 

They also teach many other **ceremonies** <term>Ceremonias</term> for this purpose; likewise, they give strange names that do not exist, for they are not truly named so. Although they all have names that distinguish them from one another, one spirit will announce another's name to his followers, and they even vex one another. This is done so that the shadow might be accepted. Whatever is seen in these things—whether figures or images—is regarded as if a favorable, kind spirit produced it; such a person sees much where there is nothing or very little. 

It is no wonder that the spirits do this. If **God** <term>Gott</term> permitted them, they would be constantly among us, even to the point of leaving us standing with our mouths agape and leading us to forget God. Therefore, such **arts** <term>künst</term> are for- <note>The text breaks off; likely "forbidden" (*verboten*) or "damned" (*verdammt*)</note>

<note>original Latin: *PRAESAGAE*</note>

...ruin of body and soul, of honor, of health, and wealth, and is nothing but meddlesome curiosity. There are two kinds of **augury** <term>Auguria</term> <note>The practice of interpreting omens from the behavior of birds or other natural signs</note>; the first is natural and just, the other stems from **spirits** <term>Geistern</term> who insert themselves into this matter, <margin>Auguries are of Nature and of spirits.</margin> for they can leave no truth untainted. The natural signs are of many kinds: A **peacock** <term>Pfaw</term> <margin>Peacock <note>Latin: *Pauo*</note></margin> screeching at unnatural times indicates and signifies a death in the house where it is, etc. For just as by God’s design the **vulture** <term>Geir</term> <margin>Vulture <note>Latin: *Vultur*</note></margin> sees carrion across the sea and darts toward it when seen, so too has He given the peacock a "scent" for carrion in the place where it is about to occur. And such things are many. 

Likewise, **ravens** <term>Raben</term> <margin>Raven <note>Latin: *Coruus*</note></margin> have an innate nature to cry out over a **plague** <term>Pestem</term> that is not yet manifest; and he who learns to recognize such crying can announce a coming **plague** <term>pestem</term>, **pleurisy** <term>pleurisin</term> <note>An inflammation of the lungs and chest cavity, often used historically to describe various respiratory ailments</note>, etc. This is natural, for birds often have the ability to recognize a thing that the human whom it affects does not, because these things are innate to them in their nature. Psittacus.*</note></margin> with its sight, the vulture smells beyond all others, and the **mole** <term>Schermauß</term> <note>Literally "shear-mouse," referring here to a mole or shrew</note> hears beyond all others. The **ape** <term>Aff</term> feels the pulse in all veins better than a human; where it detects death or a foul sickness, it snorts into its nose. So too the **parrots** <term>Sittich</term> have a sense of taste beyond all others...

Every plague is preceded by remarkable fertility. <note>original Latin: *Omnis Pestis preludiũ est fertilitas insignis.*</note>

...fruitfulness <note>The text begins mid-word: *[Fruchtbar]keit*</note> breaks out, just as summer brings all the blossoms out of the trees. If it does not all come out, then the Astral Influence <term>Influenz; the celestial or spiritual forces believed to affect health and nature</term> has no more enemy to resist it. Then the Influence holds its dominion according to its pleasure; for there is no strength left between health and sickness to oppose the Influence. One must yield, and the other must strike. Whichever yields, all the goodness within it first comes forth and must depart, just as when someone flees from his house before an enemy of the Plague <note>original: *Pestten*</note> and carries everything away with him, etc. So, when Nature foresees the Great Dying <term>Sterben; a common term for a plague or mass mortality event</term>, it grants a more fruitful year than is the custom or the nature of the land. Once all that vitality is gone, the bodies <note>original: *corpora*</note> stand weakened, and no one resists; they are too weak and cannot resist <note>original Latin: *non possunt resistere*</note>. Now the Death enters. For this reason, many and large fish often signal evil to come, as do too many "good" years; these are very certain Omens <term>Auguria; the ancient practice of interpreting omens</term>. Similarly, when there are excessive, deep snows that have never been heard of before, excessive great summers and heat, or excessive great winds, floods, etc., such that people say, "the like has never been," or at least rarely—then it should be understood that great misery, need, famine <note>original: *theurung*, referring to the "dearness" or high price of food during scarcity</note>, war, and death are coming.

<margin>
All excesses are dangerous. <note>original Latin: *Omnes excessus periculosi.*</note>
</margin>
For these things pour themselves out too much in their moisture, just as when someone overworks himself in his strength so that he must lie down and rest for several days from weakness; such a person cannot withst— <meta>catchword: withstand (widerste-)</meta>

<summary>This page explains the paradox that extreme natural abundance and record-breaking weather are actually omens of impending disaster, such as plague or war. It argues that Nature exhausts its defensive strength through these excesses, leaving humans too weak to resist the "Influence" of disease.</summary>

<margin>Divining</margin>
Divining <term>Diuiniren; the act of foretelling or guessing the future</term> is an uncertain way of prophesying / whether it happens or not / in a long or short time / this sort of prophecy is used particularly by "clever" people / who imagine themselves wiser than others / as if they know all things / and the people believe it to be so: just as a physician <term>Arzet; a doctor or healer</term> might say / the sick man will only live so long / but he lives longer / and yet dies at last / then the physician might say / he had long ago spoken of it. Divination <note>original Latin: *Diuinatio*</note> takes its basis from certain natural things / signs and arts / but it fails because there is too little experience / even though they believe they know it very well: as if someone had a certain knowledge of coming rain from Astronomy <note>original Latin: *ex Astronomia*</note> / but he did not understand that the sun might have hindered it or not / yet he says it / and it rains a long time later / etc. Then he would say / I spoke of the rain four weeks ago / I wonder it did not come sooner / just so a...

### FORETELLINGS <note>original Latin: PRAESAGAE</note>

<p align="center">D iii</p>

<meta>catchword: because-</meta>

<margin>Prophet.</margin>
A prophet is one who is dear to God, for God speaks much through him. Thus, natural prophecy proceeds **from nature** <note>original Latin: *ex natura*</note>; afterwards, we know it through experience, just as the summer is recognized by the blossoms. It is not that I say **generally** <note>original Latin: *generaliter*</note>, "it is now winter, and after that a summer will come," for I do not yet know how early or late in the month it arrives; however, I know that well by the blossoms. Likewise, when an eclipse of the sun occurs, we know through experience that a time of distress <note>original: *noht*; implying a calamity or significant hardship</note> 
<margin>Eclipses.</margin>
is approaching. Similarly, when there are various movements in the heavens, we know well that the stars in the heavens are like the trees on earth, which give their fruit and fall upon the earth. Thus rain, snow, frost, and thunder are the fruits of the stars, which grow on their branches and fall down upon the earth. Now, if we know the garden in the heavens and what trees are planted there, 
<margin>Fruits of the stars.</margin>
then we know their fruits; and yet, it is through the experience of the fruits that the trees are recognized. For an ignorant layman can recognize the pear tree by the pear, but not the other way around. And from this, through the trees and branches, we learn over time what its autumn will be and how their year is in the heavens; 
<margin>Experience for the sake of arts and oracles.</margin>
whereupon we may, from such experience, prophesy truly. For experience has produced the art over a long period of time; for experience is the first cornerstone and foundation of art,

# PREDICTIONS <note>original Latin: PRAESAGAE</note>

...divination by lots <note>original Latin: *Sortilegijs*</note> or *auguries* <note>the practice of interpreting omens from the flight of birds</note>, for every *prediction* <note>original Latin: *Presagium*</note> should proceed from its own profession—that is, from the nature of its *subject*—and possess its own *science* <note>original Latin: *scientiam*; meaning a structured field of knowledge</note>. Thus, one can *predict* things with certainty when one takes each thing from its own kind/nature in which it exists and reveals itself. Therefore, a person should not undertake far-fetched methods when he has a much closer way available: he who can see a defect in the urine does not need to look for it in *divination by lots*, *crystals*, *beryls* <note>precious stones used for scrying</note>, *Nohoch* <note>a term for a specific form of divination, likely of superstitious origin</note>, etc., as the soothsayers are accustomed to do. By doing so, you turn away from the truth toward spirits that enjoy lying; you must not *affirm* or present anything certain from such an art when you are instead permitted to speak from the unconcealed signs of nature. 

Likewise, <margin>*Red-beards* <note>original Latin: *Aenobarbi*. This refers to a historical folk prejudice against red-haired people, often considered untrustworthy.</note></margin> there are several other physical arts of experience. For instance, if I say, "Beware of red hair, for they have often deceived me"—that is not a certain <margin>*Palmistry* <note>original Latin: *Chiromãtia*</note></margin> art, for those with black hair do just as well. Therefore, there is another art based upon this, namely *Palmistry*, which contains within it the wisdom, skill, and nature of the human being, etc. For when a thing is made *by man* <note>original Latin: *ab homine*</note>, he also makes it with his own distinguishing mark <note>original: *zirt*; literally ornament or decoration, but here referring to the unique "signature" or character of a thing</note>. Nature does the same: whether it be a tree or something else, it gives each its own form, etc. It does the same with man in his formation, for man is divided <note>categorized or structured</note> just like a tree, a herb, etc. And just as one tree is recognized from another by its signs, so one man is recognized from another by his...

<summary>This page argues that true prophecy and diagnosis must come from "natural signs" (like those in medicine or palmistry) rather than superstitious tools like crystals or lots. It emphasizes that nature imprints unique, observable "signatures" on all things, including humans, which allow for certain knowledge.</summary>

### FROM THE BOOK OF THE ART <note>original Latin: EX LIB. ARTIS</note>

...the attached signs that a person carries upon himself. Just as it is said of trees—that this one is sweet, another rough, or another mild in its fruits, etc.—so it is with man: this one is envious, that one rough, and another mild or unchaste, etc. Because in Palmistry <term>Chiromantia; the art of reading character or fate from the palm</term> there must also be experience and knowledge of these signs. It is not enough to simply see a hand 
<margin>Cheek lines <note>The text likely refers to "hooked lines" (*Hackenlinien*) based on the context of hanging, but the margin indicates "cheek lines" (*Backenlinien*).</note></margin> 
with a hooked line and [predict] that the person will be hanged, and then conclude that because another person has the same line, they will also meet such a death. Such a conclusion is not established in Nature, but comes from the spirits <note>referring to "lying spirits" or superstitions mentioned on the previous page</note>, which does not serve our purpose here. 

Rather, one should view these or those lines in relation to the "Anatomy" <term>Anatomia; here meaning the structural and spiritual correspondence between man and the natural world</term> of the fig tree. Now I may say: this man's nature is of the fig tree—that is, he is a soft person, 
<margin>Fig-man.</margin> 
unstable, cheerful, and so on. In this art, the hand must be compared to trees or herbs; where that is not done, there will be no true art. Therefore, if the lines of the hand are compared to trees and found to match [a rough tree], then say: "This is a rough, clumsy, awkward person, of coarse manners and intellect." 

Similarly, if a hand—in its Anatomy—corresponds to the peach, then say: "This man becomes angry early and quickly." He "drags the bread in the oven" <note>a regional idiom suggesting someone who is hasty, impatient, or performs tasks in a messy, premature manner</note>, but his anger passes quickly. Furthermore, he is not pure; all his ways and words have a "stubble"—that is, a poison within him that no one can digest or bite into, 
<margin>Physiognomy <note>original: *Physion.*</note></margin> 
just as the pit in the peach is so hard that no one can bite through it.

->### PREDICTORS <note>original Latin: PRAESAGAE</note><-

<page-num>25</page-num>

...as with others of the same kind. Thus Physiognomy <term>Physionomia; the practice of assessing a person's character or personality from their outer appearance, especially the face</term> also has its art. Just as a man has an ore specimen—and since at that time there are many different things to be recognized, no two alike—one sign is good and serves much, while another is a bad sign for mining <note>original: *zubawen*, literally "to build" or "to work a mine"</note>, so that it may be certain, therefore more is required for the task. So it is with the face of man: a person may well be pious and also do much at times, yet he is not defined by a single sign that carries an evil indication. Conversely, just like the ore, the face is a mixed prophecy of all the characteristics that are within a person, whatever they may be. And just as one brings every kind of ore into the fire—smelter smoke <note>original: *hütterauch*; the volatile soot or vapors produced during smelting, used here to represent unstable elements</note>, slag, gold, silver, etc.—and examines them, so one finds what is truly there. So

<margin>Necessity proves it <note>original Latin: *Necessitas comprobat*</note></margin>

one must take all signs together in Physiognomy. In times of need, a person is tested and found out, for in necessity all things and characteristics reveal themselves. A person may present themselves as pious, mild, or truthful, etc., who yet possesses evil or unfaithful physiognomic signs, but he has them hidden.

<margin>The exploration of natures <note>original Latin: *ingeniorum exploratio*</note></margin>

Now, test him through the silver assay; lay it upon the scale, etc. Ask him for a sum of money based on the kindness and loyalty that you perceive in him—that is now his "fire." Now you will notice what is true in him or not. Likewise, if you think someone is steadfast, test him in his time of need; if he is unstable, he flies away from it like the smelter smoke. Therefore, one should look at every single sign...

# METEOROLOGICAL
# IMPRESSIONS <note>original Latin: IMPRESSIONES. In this context, an "impression" refers to the influence or physical mark left by celestial forces on the earthly realm.</note>

->Meteoric Effects, or Heavenly Weather and Phenomena.<-

## An Introduction or Preface by Theophrastus <note>Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim, better known as the physician and occultist Paracelsus</note>, concerning the Impression of the Element of Fire—that is, from the Firmament <note>the sphere of the stars and the heavens</note>.

->Chapter One <note>original Latin: Caput Primum</note>.<-

<summary>This page concludes a theological argument establishing the Book of Revelation as the ultimate tool for interpreting all prophecy before introducing Paracelsus’s treatise on meteorological phenomena and celestial influences.</summary>
<keywords>Apocalypse, Book of Revelation, Biblical interpretation, Paracelsus, Theophrastus, meteorology, firmament, element of fire, prophecy</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues from the introduction to Paracelsus's treatise on meteorology. The previous page established a theological framework for prophecy before transitioning into the physical and celestial mechanics of the natural world. This page specifically links the "Three Firsts" (Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury) to weather phenomena and critiques scholars who rely on imagination rather than direct observation of nature.</meta>

# IMPRESSIONS <note>original Latin: IMPRESSIONES. In this context, an "impression" refers to the influence or physical mark left by celestial forces on the earthly realm.</note>

As we have mentioned in the first prefaces concerning the Three Firsts <note>The *Tria Prima*: Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury, which Paracelsus believed were the foundational components of all matter</note>, all things regarding meteoric effects <note>This refers to all atmospheric phenomena, including rain, wind, and lightning, not just falling stars</note> are contained within them and are worked through them. The firmament <note>the sphere of the stars</note> and the elements reside within them, and the impressions come from them. Therefore, this same information does not need to be added to the third book of Meteorology <note>original: *Meteororum*</note>, as the first parts have already encompassed it. From this, it is to be understood that all impressions have their origin in the stars and the elements, as will be understood hereafter.

How these same impressions are born from them, we wish to recount in this Meteorology: how it is that other writers on meteors have not shared in our intention or our philosophy, and yet have written on meteorological matters <note>original: *Meteorica*</note>. This was perhaps caused by the spirit <note>original: *Geyst*</note> that invented their meteorology and, in part, aimed their thoughts toward that goal <note>Paracelsus is suggesting they were led astray by their own imaginations</note>. It is not fitting for us to be satisfied with an invented spirit or mind, nor to think to follow its accidental fictions philosophically. Rather, let nature alone provide the right spirit—that is, its foundation from certain <margin>The Philosopher</margin> experience <note>original: *Experientz*</note>. Otherwise, no philosopher <note>original: *Philosophus*</note> is born; for the philosopher is born from nature and is proven through experience, and not in the clever or sharp-witted spirit.

<meta>catchword: Spirit</meta>

<summary>Paracelsus explains that all weather and celestial "impressions" originate from the Three Firsts (Salt, Sulfur, Mercury) and the stars. He critiques previous scholars for using their imagination and asserts that a true philosopher must be shaped by the direct experience of nature.</summary>
<keywords>Three Firsts, Tria Prima, Meteorology, Impressions, Firmament, Experience vs. Imagination, Paracelsus, Philosophy of Nature</keywords>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues Paracelsus’s critique of previous scholars who wrote on meteorology. Having previously stated that atmospheric "impressions" arise from the Three Firsts (Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury), he now argues that true knowledge of these phenomena must come from direct experience rather than the "spirit of fantasy" or idle imagination.</meta>

# METEOROLOGY

The spirit of fantasy, which plays its pastimes within its own imagination, [is its own distraction]. One who wishes to be a natural philosopher <note>original: *Physicus*; in this era, a *physicus* was a student of nature and the physical world, distinct from a modern physician or physicist</note> should have a friendly outlook with us, in such a way that the incorporeal spirits are no different toward us than masters are over their servants and people. It should be understood thus: the masters create and command, and that command must be acted upon; thus the servants are as nothing, acting as the masters' fools. For every man would gladly be a master himself and not a master's fool, but so long as that fate <note>original: *verhengen*; referring to the divine or natural order of things</note> exists, so too are there fools.

Thus the incorporeal spirits set themselves up as masters over us, and in this we become fools—that is, we use wisdom, art, and reason invented out of our own fantasy <margin>Experience.</margin> without any Experience <note>original: *Experientz*. Paracelsus emphasizes *Experientia*—direct, hands-on observation—as the only source of true knowledge</note>, which ought to be the mother of our reason, wisdom, and art. In that case, we are the spirits' fools; they toy with us just as a master speaks whatever he wishes to his fools.

And likewise, the wise man <margin>A wise man.</margin> is to be praised who seeks his wisdom from experience; but he is to be despised who makes himself wise [by his own decree], for the spirits rule over their fools. Thus, regarding this, we may well confess that we have not been inside the oven <note>the *athanor* or chemical furnace</note> or the pot <note>the *hafen* or laboratory vessel</note> from which we see the impressions <note>In Paracelsian thought, weather phenomena are like chemical reactions occurring in a giant vessel; to understand the "impression" (the storm or wind), one must understand the "vessel" of the world</note> emerging. Will it not now be a necessity to travel? Just as we [move] from one city to another, we [must also travel through the knowledge of nature]...

<meta>catchword: be-</meta>

<summary>Paracelsus warns that scholars who rely on imagination (fantasy) rather than direct observation (experience) are merely "fools" to spirits. He argues that understanding weather "impressions" requires a practical, almost alchemical knowledge of nature's inner workings.</summary>
<keywords>fantasy vs. experience, natural philosopher, incorporeal spirits, alchemy, atmospheric impressions, empiricism</keywords>

...is sufficient that we recognize the Lower Nature, which serves as a mirror of a certain Experience <note>original: *Experientz*</note> that teaches us the essence and property of the Higher Nature. Therefore, it is rightly recognized by us that we should philosophize about meteors <note>original: *Meteorissiren*; to study or theorize about atmospheric events by moving from one observation to another</note> from one experience into the other, for the lower things signify and indicate the others. Thus, it is right for us to describe all Impressions, one after another, according to their origin, as will be shown.

## Chapter Two <note>original: *Caput Secundum*</note>

<margin>Elements</margin>
And although we have established how God set the elements out of nothing and made them, as a God, we wish to philosophize about where He placed that which was previously in that place and occupied the space. Accordingly, we shall further describe the Element of Air in the place where the state of the four elements existed, which was cast out from there into Hell, and occupied the place together with its retinue <note>A reference to the fall of Lucifer and the rebellious angels, who were "cast out" to make room for the physical creation</note>.
